# Data provenance

Every numeric dataset in this tree gets a row here: element masses, fit
coefficients, optical data, experimental points. A dataset without a row is a
CI-review blocker.

The allowed origins are:

1. **Computed from a published formula.** Cite the paper; the code is ours.
2. **Experimental measurement.** Cite the paper or database record.
   Measurements are facts; we cite them.
3. **A permissive source** (Tier A in [`../CONTRIBUTING.md`](../CONTRIBUTING.md)),
   with its notice kept in `THIRD_PARTY_LICENSES.md`.

The following are **never allowed**:

- SRIM tables, including any that other projects pass along.
- ICRU report tables.
- NIST SRD tables, until their terms have been reviewed and recorded here.
- Anything whose origin is not known.

**Printed sources and scans.** Cite the printed edition (author, title,
edition, publisher, year, ISBN, table and page). A scan may be used to read
values, but it is never linked from this repo and its location or upload
identifier is not recorded: a row says only that a scan of the printed edition
was read, plus the SHA-256 of the file, so anyone holding the same file can
check the reading. A copy published openly by the work's own publisher or
issuing institution is not a third-party scan and may be linked as the source.

Interval elements (H, Li, B, C, N, O, Mg, Si, S, Cl, Ar, Br, Tl, Pb, 14 in all; the old module docs omitted Ar and Pb): the single value is the abridged value of <https://www.ciaaw.org/abridged-atomic-weights.htm>; the 2021 report (Table 1, columns 7-8, and the text above it; read in its author manuscript, BNL-221671-2021-JAAM, <https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/1798495>) says this column replaces and equals the former conventional atomic weights, except H, printed 1.0080 for the former 1.008. Agree as stored (33): Li, B, P, S, K, Ca, Sc (2.989 at 25 °C: the reviewer flag is resolved for this edition, value unchanged), Ti, V, Fe, Co, Cu, Ga, Ge, Rb, Y, Zr, Nb, Rh, Cd, In, I, Ce, Sm, Tb, Er, Lu, Hf, Re, Pt, Tl, Th, Pa. Si: kept at 2.329; the booklet prints 2.33 (25 °C), and 2.329 is the crystal density M/V_m from the CODATA 2022 "molar volume of silicon" 1.205 883 199e-5 m³/mol (<https://physics.nist.gov/cuu/Constants/Table/allascii.txt>; equal to N_A a³/8 for the listed lattice parameter) for any weight in [28.084, 28.086]. **Checked 2026-10-07 (#15) against other open primary compilations** (U.S. government reports; the booklet does not pin these four): **Cr corrected 7.15 to 7.20 g/cm³**: the upper end of the booklet's printed range 7.18-7.20 (the old value lay outside it), and "the density of alpha chromium calculated from the NBS lattice constant is 7.200 at 25° C" (an X-ray crystal density, a = 2.8839 Å) in H. E. Swanson, R. K. Fuyat and G. M. E. Swanson and E. Tatge, NBS Circular 539, Vol. I (1953), p. 33 (PDF page 37; <https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/circ/nbscircular539v1.pdf>, SHA-256 `003b872fbe7d403d38bbd1d752568ee92a96bdb010b6e381bdc82566929e2a6c`); note that recomputing from its printed a = 4.0786 Å with CODATA 2022 N_A and the CIAAW weight gives 19.283, so the printed 19.302 is taken as printed and the stored second decimal rests on it. **Ra 5.0 confirmed** (resolves the reviewer flag): "It has a specific gravity of approximately 5.0" in H. W. Kirby and M. L. Salutsky, *The Radiochemistry of Radium*, NAS-NS 3057 (National Academy of Sciences, 1964), p. 3 (PDF page 14), citing the International Critical Tables (1928-1933) (<https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/4560824>, doi:10.2172/4560824, SHA-256 `f20a8eb8ec1abdec7e79b6f9bde6e10a5e4b1029fa34270423cf0000436bb8b1`); approximate, so only to its printed precision. **Ac 10.07 confirmed**: fcc actinium metal, a = 5.311 +- 0.010 Å, crystal density d = 10.07 g/cc, Table 4, p. 11 (PDF page 12) of J. D. Farr, A. L. Giorgi, M. G. Bowman and R. K. Money, "The crystal structure of actinium metal and actinium hydride", Los Alamos report LA-1545 (1953) (<https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/4397640>, doi:10.2172/4397640, SHA-256 `f27bb13e26433ee86bee1d6a97cb125d5bd32e8542a4f6d5134e3f0f510be35c`), published as J. Inorg. Nucl. Chem. 18, 42 (1961); 4 x 227 / (N_A a³) = 10.065. These are X-ray or approximate values, not 20 °C specific gravities; they are used because the booklet gives none (Ra, Ac) or only a range or "~" (Cr, Au). Not found in these sources: Mn metal and graphite are absent from NBS Circular 539 Vols. I-X (cumulative index, Vol. X). **Mn cross-checked 2026-10-07 (#15), not pinned**: the cumulative index of NBS Monograph 25, Section 20 (<https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/MONO/nbsmonograph25-20.pdf>) lists alpha-Mn in Section 17, p. 50; there, M. C. Morris, H. F. McMurdie, E. H. Evans and B. Paretzkin, *Standard X-ray Diffraction Powder Patterns*, NBS Monograph 25, Section 17 (October 1980), p. 50 (PDF page 56; <https://nvlpubs.nist.gov/nistpubs/Legacy/MONO/nbsmonograph25-17.pdf>, SHA-256 `328a91de995b1e3d8e32fe8a2f7932c74dc4949ccb1a8ba4652e3ab5cf2dde00`), prints for alpha-Mn at 25 ± 1 °C: cubic I-43m, Z = 58, a = 8.9121(4) Å, volume 707.85 Å³, "Density (calculated) 7.475 g/cm³"; it also prints the alpha-beta transition at about 700 °C (from Sully 1955), so alpha is the room-temperature form. Recomputed with CODATA 2022 N_A and the CIAAW weight: 7.4750. That X-ray density lies above the booklet's printed range 7.21-7.44, so, unlike Cr, it does not select a value inside the range; the stored 7.44 is unchanged and still unverified. The same index has no graphite entry (only diamond, Section 2), and a 2025 OSTI report on nuclear graphite (OSTI 3002552) gives only a nominal 2.26. **Still unverified** (unchanged): C 2.267 (printed only as the range 1.9-2.3, graphite) and Mn 7.44 (the upper end of the printed range 7.21-7.44). The booklet names no allotrope except graphite; the earlier claims "white P, alpha-S, white Sn" are not verified. `None` stays for gases, the liquids Br and Hg (the booklet lists their liquid densities; the table holds solid densities only), At and Fr. Validation note: Ag is the only changed element in a committed validation input; the level-3 Ag sputter yields (`validation/experiments/results.json`) and the RustBCA Ag context summary were computed at 10.49 and are not regenerated here. | Default surface binding energy `E_s` (cohesive energy) | `lindhard/src/elements.rs` | Experimental (thermochemistry) | C. Kittel, *Introduction to Solid State Physics*, 8th ed. (John Wiley & Sons, 2005, ISBN 0-471-41526-X), chapter 3 "Crystal binding and elastic constants", **Table 1 "Cohesive energies", p. 50**: "Energy required to form separated neutral atoms in their ground electronic state from the solid at 0 K at 1 atm. The data were supplied by Prof. Leo Brewer." Each element is printed in kJ/mol, eV/atom and kcal/mol; the eV/atom value is used, to the digits printed. Use as `E_s` is the Sigmund 1969 convention (Phys. Rev. 184, 383). Convention, not a measured surface barrier. **Verified 2026-10-07 (#15)**: the cited source is the printed book, Kittel 8th ed., Table 1, p. 50. The values were read from a scan of the printed 8th edition (not a publisher copy; per the scan rule above it is not linked), PDF SHA-256 `7c1c24f27972203af47adb3c16f2ac136ad31387ee566be4a1ffb80c8cde9d01`, PDF page 71 = printed page 50. Edition, publisher, year and ISBN were read from its title and copyright pages, and the index entry "Cohesive energy, table, 50" in the publisher's free index excerpt (`047141526X-3.pdf`, SHA-256 `68cf81b875e846e382ea04e9a29dd4d460c2d0dfc894dfe473dfc20b387e6d43`, under <https://media.wiley.com/product_data/excerpt/6X/04714152/>) matches the page. The transcription was checked for internal consistency: for all 35 populated elements the printed kJ/mol and kcal/mol values, converted at 96.4853 kJ/mol per eV and 4.184 J/cal, agree with the printed eV/atom value to within 0.01 eV. **All 35 populated entries compared**: 33 equal the printed value (Cr 4.1, Ta 8.1 and W 8.9 equal the printed 4.10, 8.10, 8.90); **corrected**: Na 1.11 to 1.113 eV, K 0.93 to 0.934 eV (stored with fewer digits than printed). This also resolves the earlier Brewer cross-check discrepancies: Li 1.63, Mg 1.51 and B 5.81 are the values Kittel prints. Table 1 also prints values for elements left unset here (N, O, F, Ne, P, S, Cl, Ar, Sc, Mn, Se, Br, Kr, Rb, Sr, Y, Tc, Ru, Rh, Te, I, Xe, Cs, Ba, La, Ce-Lu except Pm, Hf, Re, Os, Ir, Hg, Tl, Bi, Po, Rn, Ra, Ac, Th, U); those defaults stay `None` (#15 audits populated entries and keeps missing defaults unset); H, He, Pm, At, Fr and Pa have no value in the table. Earlier cross-check, kept: L. Brewer, "The cohesive energies of the elements", report LBL-3720 (1975), Table I, pp. 4-9, kcal/gram-atom (<https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/7187973>). Tests: `elements::tests::surface_binding_matches_kittel_8th_ed_table_1` (all 35 populated values, including Si, Ga, As, B, Au; O and P unset), `elements::tests::surface_binding_cross_checks_against_brewer_lbl_3720` | Facts, cited | 2026-10-04 |
| Default displacement energy `E_d` | `lindhard/src/elements.rs` | Convention | ASTM E521 recommended values (the NRT convention; Norgett, Robinson, Torrens, Nucl. Eng. Des. 33, 50 (1975)). A convention for damage accounting; **not yet verified** against the standard; check each value before relying on it. Missing elements are unset by design. Access attempted 2026-10-06 (#15): astm.org answers 403 to the standard's page; no edition is open. ORNL/LTR-2026/4378 (OSTI 3028686), a report on the E521 revision, contains no displacement-energy table; the open copy of Stoller et al., NIM B 310, 75 (2013) listed by OSTI (1132718, 1086644) returns 404. Further attempts 2026-10-07 (#15): <https://www.oecd-nea.org/science/docs/2015/nsc-doc2015-9.pdf> (NEA/NSC/DOC(2015)9) and <https://www-nds.iaea.org/publications/indc/indc-nds-0648.pdf> return HTML pages, not the reports; Nordlund et al., J. Nucl. Mater. 512, 450 (2018) (OSTI 1482433) has no ASTM E521 table (its Table 5 lists arc-dpa fit parameters with their own `E_d`, which are not the E521 values); OSTI 3030328, a second 2026 report on the E521 revision, has no displacement-energy table; OSTI 1132718 and 1086644 (Stoller et al. 2013) still return 404; the Elsevier API full text of Konobeyev et al., Nucl. Energy Technol. 3, 169 (2017), doi:10.1016/j.nucet.2017.08.007, returns 400. Further attempts 2026-10-07 (#15, third pass): Konobeyev et al. (2017), above, read in full from the KIT repository copy (<https://publikationen.bibliothek.kit.edu/1000076502/4841844>, CC BY-NC-ND): it does not cite ASTM E521 and has no E521 table; Nordlund et al., Nat. Commun. 9, 1084 (2018), doi:10.1038/s41467-018-03415-5: no E521 table; Shao et al., NIM B (2017), doi:10.1016/j.nimb.2017.05.026 (author manuscript LA-UR-17-25080, OSTI 1415407): cites E521 but has no table; a 2022 review (doi:10.54565/jphcfum.1027393) states only Fe 40 eV as an example "from ASTM E521", a tertiary statement not counted as verification; <https://www.oecd-nea.org/upload/docs/application/pdf/2019-12/nsc-doc2015-9.pdf> returns 404 and the NEA and IAEA URLs above now return 403; the OSTI records of Stoller et al. 2013 (1086644, 1132718) offer no full text, and the article is closed (OpenAlex). Copies of the standard on document-sharing sites found by a web search are unauthorized and were not used. Further attempts 2026-10-07 (#15, fourth pass): scans of *Annual Book of ASTM Standards* 1991, Vol. 12.02 "Nuclear" and Vol. 12.01 were found, but both are access-restricted (lend-only), which edition of E521 they print was not seen, and the search-inside service answers "Item not available" for Vol. 12.02; Stoller et al. (2013) is closed in OpenAlex and Semantic Scholar. Two open compilations list `E_d` values without attributing them to E521, so they are **not** E521 evidence: L. R. Greenwood and R. K. Smither, *SPECTER: Neutron Damage Calculations for Materials Irradiations*, ANL/FPP/TM-197 (1985), Table II, p. 14 (<https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/6022143>, SHA-256 `489bca1482ffb33c160329283c0bbb2245841d2808458749ff431caf8d89ce44`), and the NJOY2016 manual, LA-UR-17-20093, Table 2, p. 120 (<https://www.osti.gov/servlets/purl/1338791>, SHA-256 `aa6d51e6fb4bed9260a2e8abec3dbd6ea33302dc95c5fb94db958609d3dd7af8`), which cites Greenwood and Smither (IAEA meeting, 1981). The stored values are not that compilation either: NJOY's Table 2 has Al 27, Ti 40, Cu 40, Nb 40, Ag 60 where this table has 25, 30, 30, 60, 30 (the other 11 populated entries agree with it; SPECTER also prints Ta 53). Nothing was changed on that basis. Further attempts 2026-10-07 (#15, fifth pass): an Internet Archive full-text search for "E521" and for threshold displacement energies found no copy of the standard, and an OSTI search for "ASTM E521" with "displacement energies" returned only papers that cite single values from E521 (tertiary, not counted). Gap: the cited edition (year not recorded) and the 16 populated values remain unchecked. Entered from the contributor's memory of the paper and **not verified**; spot-check against the paper before relying on it. The status is also the constant `OR_CONSTANTS_UNVERIFIED` in the same file, and crystal runs that use the local loss carry `electronic_constants_unverified: true` in their metadata (`CrystalMetadata`, #226); verifying the constants (#251) flips that one constant. The crystal-versus-amorphous validation of #226 (`lindhard/tests/crystal_electronic.rs`) compares two runs that use the same constants, so it tests the crystal geometry, not the constants: a wrong value largely cancels in the crystal/amorphous ratio. Absolute electronic losses and channeled ranges computed with `EquipartitionLsOr` do depend on them | Formula, cited | 2026-10-04 |

| Single-oscillator density effect `δ = ln((βγ)²(ħω_p/I)²) − 1 + (I/ħω_p)²/(βγ)²` (no fitted constants; uses CODATA ħ, e, m_e) | `lindhard/src/ion/stopping/bethe.rs` | Computed from a published formula | Fermi, Phys. Rev. 57, 485 (1940); Sternheimer, Phys. Rev. 88, 851 (1952); Fano, Ann. Rev. Nucl. Sci. 13, 1 (1963): the dielectric-model `δ = Σ f_i ln(1 + L²/ω_i²) − L²(1−β²)/(β²ω_p²)`. The one-oscillator closed form is our own algebraic reduction, checked in unit tests against its limits (zero at threshold in value and slope; `2 ln(βγ ħω_p/I) − 1` at high energy). Equation numbers and the source formula's exact form were not verified against the papers (recalled; the reduction reproduces the standard high-energy limit, which is the check). Plasma energy of Al checked against 32.86 eV | Formula, cited | 2026-10-05 |
| Proton-in-Si Bethe hand-calculation values (1, 10, 100 MeV) | `lindhard/src/ion/stopping/bethe.rs` (tests) | Computed from the Bethe formula with `I = 140 eV` and CODATA constants | Our own arithmetic; not taken from PSTAR/ICRU/SRIM | Computed | 2026-10-04 |
| Screening function coefficients (ZBL universal, Kr-C, Moliere, Lenz-Jensen) | `lindhard/src/ion/potential.rs` | Computed from published formulas | ZBL: Ziegler, Biersack, Littmark (1985) Ch. 2 (exponents 3.2, 0.9423, 0.4029, 0.2016); Kr-C: Wilson, Haggmark, Biersack, Phys. Rev. B 15, 2458 (1977); Moliere: Z. Naturforsch. A 2, 133 (1947); Lenz-Jensen: Lenz, Z. Phys. 77, 713 (1932), Jensen, Z. Phys. 77, 722 (1932), polynomial form as tabulated in Moller (2017) (see below; the earlier pointer to Nastasi, Mayer, Hirvonen (1996) was never checked). Entered from the contributor's memory; the primary sources have not been re-read digit by digit. Cross-checked 2026-10-05 against `include/screened_coulomb.h` of the MIT-licensed `ir2-lab/screened_coulomb` (OpenTRIM component, commit f84c3c8), a secondary source: Kr-C and Moliere agree digit for digit; ZBL agrees to the printed rounding (that source carries 0.18175, 0.50986, 0.28022, 0.028171 / 3.19980, 0.94229, 0.40290, 0.20162). Independent consistency: the ZBL set reproduces the separately published ZBL stopping fit to 0.39 % for eps <= 0.3 (`stopping_vs_zbl_universal_fit_low_energy`). **Lenz-Jensen, 2026-10-05 (#45): agrees with a textbook tabulation, still unverified against the primary papers.** All five polynomial coefficients (1, 1, 0.3344, 0.0485, 0.002647) and the scale 9.67 appear exactly so in W. Moller, *Fundamentals of Ion-Solid Interaction*, HZDR-073 (2017), p. 11, eq. (28): `phi_LJ(xi) = (1 + xi + 0.3344 xi^2 + 0.0485 xi^3 + 0.002647 xi^4) e^-xi`, `xi = sqrt(9.67 r/a)` (seen at https://www.hzdr.de/publications/PublDoc-10091.pdf). This is a secondary source. Its `a` is the single-atom Thomas-Fermi length `0.8853 a0 Z^(-1/3)` (eq. (26)), and the two-atom form uses the Lindhard length (eqs. (29)-(30)), as in our `default_length`. Two of its printed values differ from ours, and neither difference is established as an error. Eq. (27) prints the Moliere exponents as 0.3/0.2/6, while ours is 1.2 for the middle one; our value is backed only by the Tier A cross-check, and the primary source was not consulted. Eq. (33) prints the universal prefactor as 0.8853, while other secondary sources print 0.8854 (see the screening-lengths row); the primary source (ZBL 1985) was not consulted. So this source is not treated as decisive. No source was found for the alternative scale `3.11126^2 = 9.680`. One further secondary form, `x = 4.5397 Z^(1/6) r^(1/2)` with r in angstrom (Science World Journal, https://www.scienceworldjournal.org/article/download/11618/7859/0, eq. 19, citing Bister and Hautala, Phys. Lett. A 68, 98 (1978)), converts with `a_TF = 0.8853 a0 Z^(-1/3)` to a scale of 9.655, 0.15 % below 9.67. The value is unchanged. Not seen: Lenz (doi:10.1007/BF01342150), Jensen (doi:10.1007/BF01342151) and Bister and Hautala (doi:10.1016/0375-9601(78)90770-3) are all closed access (Unpaywall). Nastasi-Mayer-Hirvonen, Eckstein (1991) and Gombas could not be reached either (no open copy on archive.org; Google Books API out of quota). Web searches for "Lenz-Jensen" with 0.3344, 0.002647, 9.67 and 3.11126 found nothing else | Formulas, cited | 2026-10-04 |
| Screening lengths (universal, Firsov, Lindhard) | `lindhard/src/ion/potential.rs` | Computed from published formulas | 0.8853 is computed as (9 pi^2/128)^(1/3). Status 2026-10-05 (#45). **Firsov, verified against the primary source**: O. B. Firsov, Sov. Phys. JETP 6, 534 (1958), p. 535 (below eq. (4)), `a = (9 pi^2/128)^(1/3) hbar^2/(m e^2) = 4.68e-9 cm`, and p. 536, `x' = (Z1^(1/2) + Z2^(1/2))^(2/3) R/a` (seen at http://www.jetp.ras.ru/cgi-bin/dn/e_006_03_0534.pdf). **Lindhard, verified against the primary source**: Lindhard, Scharff, Schiott, Mat. Fys. Medd. Dan. Vid. Selsk. 33(14) (1963), p. 8, `a = a0 . 0.8853 (Z1^(2/3) + Z2^(2/3))^(-1/2)` (seen at http://gymarkiv.sdu.dk/MFM/kdvs/mfm%2030-39/mfm-33-14.pdf). Test: `screening_length_prefactors_match_primary_sources`. **Universal (0.8854 a0 / (Z1^0.23 + Z2^0.23), ZBL 1985 Ch. 2), unverified against the primary source**: the 1985 book was not accessible (no open copy; srim.org offers only the table of contents and index of its 2008 successor). Secondary sources agree on the exponent 0.23 and split on the prefactor: 0.8854 in the TU Wien theses of A. Hossinger (eq. (3.92), https://www.iue.tuwien.ac.at/phd/hoessinger/node39.html) and R. Wittmann (eq. (3.14), https://www.iue.tuwien.ac.at/phd/wittmann/node8.html); 0.8853 in Moller, HZDR-073 (2017), eq. (33); and the MIT-licensed `ir2-lab/screened_coulomb` (Tier A, commit f84c3c8) uses its Thomas-Fermi constant 0.88534. The spread is 1e-4 relative, so the value is unchanged | Formulas, cited | 2026-10-04 |
| Magic-formula constants C1..C5 (ZBL and Moliere sets) | `lindhard/src/ion/scattering.rs` | Computed from published formula | Biersack and Haggmark, Nucl. Instrum. Methods 174 (1980) 257; ZBL (1985) Ch. 2. Entered from memory. ZBL set (0.99229, 0.011615, 0.0071222, 14.813, 9.3066) and the formula itself (`gamma = (C4+eps)/(C5+eps)`, `G = gamma/(sqrt(1+A^2)-A)`, denominator `x0 + rho`): cross-checked 2026-10-05 against `include/screened_coulomb.h` of the MIT-licensed `ir2-lab/screened_coulomb` (OpenTRIM component, commit f84c3c8), a secondary source. Moliere set (0.6743, 0.009611, 0.005175, 10.0, 6.314): **still unverified** (re-checked 2026-10-05, #45). Biersack and Haggmark (doi:10.1016/0029-554X(80)90440-1) is closed access (Unpaywall), and no permissively licensed copy exists. Open secondary texts checked without finding the set: Moller, HZDR-073 (2017), which names the magic formula but gives no constants, and the TU Wien theses of Hossinger, Wittmann and Bohmayr. Web searches for 0.6743 and 0.009611 with "magic formula" or "Biersack" found nothing. Functional check: against the quadrature, worst `|d cos(theta/2)|` is 0.0060 (Moliere) and 0.016 (ZBL), and the Moliere check rejects the ZBL set (0.015) and a C4/C5 swap (0.07 to 0.09) (`magic_formula_check_rejects_wrong_constants`) | Formulas, cited | 2026-10-04 |
| ZBL universal reduced nuclear stopping fit (test reference only) | `lindhard/src/ion/scattering.rs` (tests); copied into `lindhard/tests/validation/scattering.rs` | Published fit formula | ZBL (1985) Ch. 2: `ln(1+1.1383 eps) / (2 (eps + 0.01321 eps^0.21226 + 0.19593 eps^0.5))`, `eps <= 30`; `ln(eps)/(2 eps)` above. The `eps <= 30` coefficients were cross-checked 2026-10-05 against `include/screened_coulomb.h` of the MIT-licensed `ir2-lab/screened_coulomb` (OpenTRIM component, commit f84c3c8), a secondary source. Our quadrature agrees to 0.39 % for eps <= 0.3. Above that it deviates by up to 5.5 % (near eps = 30), for a known reason. The `eps > 30` branch is the high-energy asymptote `(ln eps + C)/(2 eps)` with C dropped. C = 0.2744 for the ZBL potential, from the first-order momentum (impulse) approximation matched to Rutherford. That approximation is eq. (3.1)-(3.4) of Lindhard, Nielsen, Scharff, Mat. Fys. Medd. Dan. Vid. Selsk. 36(10) (1968), p. 12 (verified against http://gymarkiv.sdu.dk/MFM/kdvs/mfm%2030-39/mfm-36-10.pdf, 2026-10-05, #45; the quadrature reproduces it to 5e-6 at eps = 1e5, `small_angles_match_lns_perturbation_formula`). The earlier credit to Lehmann and Leibfried, Z. Phys. 172, 465 (1963), is kept only as a pointer to higher-order corrections. Its title, "Higher order momentum approximations in classical collision theory" (doi:10.1007/BF01378911), was confirmed via Crossref; the text is closed access and was not seen. Dropping C underestimates s_n by C/ln(eps): 4.0 % at 1e3, 1.5 % at 1e8, which the quadrature reproduces. The quadrature matches that asymptote to 0.10 % at eps = 1e3 (`stopping_matches_high_energy_asymptote`) | Formula, cited | 2026-10-04 |

The masses in `ELEMENTS` are the standard atomic weights of `lindhard/src/elements.rs` (row above), checked at run time against lindhard's echoed density and atom density | Inputs | 2026-10-05 |

Each file holds scalar summary metrics only (range moments, yields, ions/s, their differences and standard errors), the settings of both sides and every mismatch. Phys. paper, which could not be opened (row above; Crossref lists only the paper's own 34 references and the Zenodo files, including `Energy.xlsx`, carry no list). Its sets that are not in Joy (2001) therefore cannot be cited to an original measurement and are **not stored**. Those with points in 1 to 30 keV (Akbari's per-table reference numbers): C 1 'non-cleaned sample', 4, 21; Al 1 'non-cleaned sample', 4, 7, 23; Si 24; Cu 1 'non-cleaned sample', 2, 4, 7; Au 1 'non-cleaned sample', 7, 24 (the 'cleaned sample' companion of each set 1 is Joy's El Gomati and Assad set). Phys. paper's reference list can add them with their originals | Gap | 2026-10-07 |

| Weak-collision annuli `p_k = p_max sqrt(k + R)`, `k = 1..K`, `K <= 3`; weak collisions make no recoils | `lindhard/src/ion/bca/mod.rs` (`BcaConfig::weak_collisions`, `MAX_WEAK_COLLISIONS`); `lindhard/tests/validation/lss.rs` and `partition.rs` (the same rings in the reference solutions) | Published formula and convention | W. Moller and W. Eckstein, *TRIDYN - Binary collision simulation of atomic collisions and dynamic composition changes in solids*, report IPP 9/64, Max-Planck-Institut fur Plasmaphysik (1988), the long write-up of Comput. Phys. Commun. 51 (1988) 355 (doi:10.1016/0010-4655(88)90148-8, closed access): eq. (26) and "up to three additional weak collisions", p. 14; Fig. 3, p. 11 (first-order partner at its own azimuth); weak collision loop before the hard collision, new direction after each, a recoil only for the hard collision, p. 26; summed elastic and local inelastic transfers, p. 35; a weak partner beyond the surface is skipped ("Target beyond Surf.?" inside the weak-collision loop) and the elastic loss is applied once after the loop, Figs. 7 and 8, pp. 29-30. **Verified against the source**: scanned report read at <https://pure.mpg.de/rest/items/item_2131703/component/file_2131702/content> on 2026-10-05 (physics and program-description sections only; its Fortran listing, Appendix 1, was not read). Deliberate deviations, documented and measured in the module docs: each collision is evaluated at the energy left after the previous one, not at the step's starting energy as Figs. 7 and 8 and p. 35 imply, because the summed transfers would then often exceed a slow atom's energy and the report gives no rule for that; and the surface test is applied to the weak collisions only, not to the hard one, so that `K = 0` is unchanged. Defaults of other codes, read in their manuals only: SDTrimSP `iwc = iwcr = 2` (Mutzke et al., *SDTrimSP Version 6.00*, IPP 2019-02, Table 13, p. 73, <https://pure.mpg.de/rest/items/item_3026474/component/file_3028154/content>); RustBCA `weak_collision_order` default 0 (wiki page "Standalone Code: Input File", Tier B manual). lindhard's default is 0 | Formula, cited | 2026-10-05 |

Nielsen, M. Scharff and P. V. Thomsen, Mat. Fys. Medd. Dan. Vid. Selsk. 33 (10) (1963): eq. (2.7), p. 14 (homogeneous part for `nu(E)`, p. 20); `a1` for approximation (D) with `s = 2` power-law scattering, p. 23, beside 1, 16/13 and 8/7 for the other approximations; Thomas-Fermi low-energy behaviour `s = 3`, p. 26. **Verified against the source**, read at <http://gymarkiv.sdu.dk/MFM/kdvs/mfm%2030-39/mfm-33-10.pdf> on 2026-10-05. The solver (implicit march, Richardson extrapolation) is our own numerics; its inputs are the engine's ZBL table and Lindhard-Scharff stopping (rows above) | Formula, cited | 2026-10-05 |

E. Cullen, and the issuing data center) carried here and in the file header. This is the operator's decision on project risk, not a legal opinion. The source states no redistribution terms, only a warranty disclaimer and the credit request (file header MF=1 MT=451, NNDC EPICS2017 page, IAEA-NDS-224 Rev. 1, IAEA-NDS-218); no warranty is made for the committed values | 2026-10-06 |
| Optical energy-loss function ELF = Im[-1/ε] of Al, far infrared to x-ray (`validation/data/optical/al_elf_hagemann1975.toml`, 148 points, 1.0e-3 eV to 1.2e5 eV) | `validation/data/optical/al_elf_hagemann1975.toml` | Experimental measurement (optical constants n, k), transcription read from an open copy and checked against the scanned tables | Measurement: H.-J. Hagemann, W. Gudat and C. Kunz, J. Opt. Soc. Am. 65, 742 (1975), doi:10.1364/JOSA.65.000742 (closed access; not seen). Full tables: DESY report SR-74/7 (May 1974), **Table 2, "Optical constants of Al"** (PDF pp. 40-42), scanned copy at <https://refractiveindex.info/download/data/1974/Hagemann%201974%20-%20DESY%20report%20SR-74-7.pdf> (linked from the transcription's own references), **seen 2026-10-07**. Rows read from `Al/nk/Hagemann.yml` of the CC0 refractiveindex.info database (commit `c5c2f18`), fetched 2026-10-07; Table 2 has the same 148 rows. **Second read** (`validation/data/optical/secondread_hagemann1975.json`): n and k read by eye from the scan at 17 energies (plasmon region 14.5 to 18 eV, 40 eV, both sides of the L₂,₃ edge at 72.9 and 73.1 eV, 100 and 500 eV, 1 keV, both sides of the K edge at 1.55 and 1.6 keV, 3, 9, 20, 50 and 120 keV) all equal the transcription. The report also prints the ELF (`ENERGYLOSS`): our ELF, formed from the printed 3- to 4-digit n, k, agrees with it within 0.5 % at 13 of the 17 energies; it differs by -1.4 % (14.5 eV), +1.5 % (15 eV) and -7.0 % (16 eV) on the plasmon peak, where ε₁ ≈ 0 makes the ELF sensitive to the rounding of n and k (at 16 eV the printed ELF, 3.14, also disagrees with the printed ε₁, ε₂ of the same row, which give 2.83), and by -10.6 % at 72.9 eV, where the printed ELF (3.50E-02, set in a different typeface from the rest of the column) disagrees with the printed ε₁ = 1.073, ε₂ = 3.61E-02 of its own row, which give 3.13E-02 as we do. **Joins** (report Section 4, "Aluminum", pp. 14-15; the authors' references in brackets): absorption from Drude parameters [44] to 0.155 eV, interpolated after [44] to 0.8 eV, ellipsometry [20, UHV] to 2.0 eV, a Drude estimate after [44] to 13.5 eV, k from reflectance at several angles and transmission [19] to 36 eV, their own UHV transmission to 74 eV (absolute values fitted at 74 eV), transmission [45] to 150 eV, transmission to 300 eV (shape from [29, 30], scale fitted to [28]), and Hubbell's compiled attenuation cross sections above 300 eV; n then by Kramers-Kronig. The authors note that their values from 200 to 500 eV "may be less accurate" and could cause the n_eff excess (p. 15). Converted by `validation/data/optical/ingest_hagemann.py`: E = hc/λ, ELF = ε₂/(ε₁²+ε₂²), ε₁ = n²-k², ε₂ = 2nk (no smoothing, joining or extrapolation by lindhard). Independent spot check against Henke et al. (CXRO `al.nff`, retrieved 2026-10-07): ε formed from **both** f1 and f2 as n = 1 - δ + iβ with δ, β = r_e λ² n_a f1,2 / 2π (n_a from the NIST density and A = Z/(Z/A) of the electron-density row below), ε₁ = (1-δ)² - β², ε₂ = 2(1-δ)β, ELF = ε₂/(ε₁²+ε₂²); between CXRO grid points f1 interpolated linearly and f2 as a power law in E, compared at this table's own knots (no interpolation of the Hagemann data). ELF ratio Hagemann/Henke: 1.53 (40 eV), 1.31 (100 eV), 1.03 (200 eV), 1.15 (500 eV), 0.98 (1 keV), 1.08 (3 keV), 1.12 (9 keV), 1.16 (20 keV); the valence-region ratios are not a test of the data, since Henke's free-atom f2 has no plasmon or band structure. **Sum rules** (`lindhard/tests/optical_sumrule.rs`), gated on the representation `OpticalElf::elf()` serves, linear in E between knots, integrated exactly: **P_eff = 1.094 and N_eff = 14.79 for Z = 13 (+13.8 %); both fail the 5 % check.** Power-law segments between the same knots, kept as a separately labelled source-side comparison, give P_eff = 0.999 and N_eff = 13.98 (+7.5 %, also pinned as a failure); the trapezoid rule gives 1.100 and 14.54 (printed, not gated). So the P_eff excess and about half of the N_eff excess (0.82 of 1.79 electrons) belong to the representation: by energy, linear minus power-law is +0.30 electrons from 10 to 30 eV (chords across the 15 eV plasmon peak, knots 0.5 eV apart) and +0.50 from 100 eV to 10 keV (sparse x-ray knots). The authors' own N_eff from the same function is 13.6 (+4.5 %, report Table 10, errata sheet); the further ~3 % between that and the power-law 13.98 is quadrature on the printed knots, measured by integrating ε₂ the same way against the report's own N-EFF column (13.89 against 13.495). Kept, with all three failures pinned in the test and explained, rather than dropped or tuned | Facts, cited, under operator ruling #34 (numbers only; a measured, converted table) | 2026-10-07 |
| Optical energy-loss function ELF = Im[-1/ε] of Cu, far infrared to x-ray (149 points, 5.0e-3 eV to 5.0e4 eV) | `validation/data/optical/cu_elf_hagemann1975.toml` | Experimental measurement (optical constants n, k), transcription read from an open copy and checked against the scanned tables | As the Al row, with `Cu/nk/Hagemann.yml` (same commit) and the report's **Table 3, "Optical constants of Cu"** (PDF pp. 43-45). Table 3 has 150 rows; the transcription omits the first (2.00E-03 eV), whose ε₁ and n are blank in the report, and has the other 149. **Second read** (same file): n and k at 12 energies (15, 16, 18, 40, 73 eV near the M₂,₃ onset, 100 and 500 eV, 1 keV above the L edge, 8 and 9 keV on both sides of the K edge, 15 and 50 keV) all equal the transcription; our ELF agrees with the report's printed ELF within 0.3 % at all 12. **Joins** (report Section 4, "Copper", pp. 15-16): absorption from Drude parameters [5] to 0.5 eV, k from reflectance and transmission [5] to 6.5 eV, an interpolated curve between two reflectance analyses [11, 12], absolute values fitted to [11], to 13 eV, their own transmission to 150 eV (with the additive correction for detector drift described on p. 13), transmission [47] to 450 eV, and Hubbell's compiled attenuation cross sections above 450 eV; n then by Kramers-Kronig. Independent spot check against Henke et al. (CXRO `cu.nff`, retrieved 2026-10-07): ε formed from **both** f1 and f2 as n = 1 - δ + iβ with δ, β = r_e λ² n_a f1,2 / 2π (n_a from the NIST density and A = Z/(Z/A) of the electron-density row below), ε₁ = (1-δ)² - β², ε₂ = 2(1-δ)β, ELF = ε₂/(ε₁²+ε₂²); between CXRO grid points f1 interpolated linearly and f2 as a power law in E, compared at this table's own knots (no interpolation of the Hagemann data). ELF ratio Hagemann/Henke: 0.87 (40 eV), 1.17 (100 eV), 0.97 (200 eV), 0.91 (500 eV), 0.90 (1 keV), 0.87 (4 keV), 0.95 (8 keV), 0.93 (15 keV), 1.00 (30 keV). **Sum rules** (gated on the linear representation `elf()` serves, exactly integrated): P_eff = 1.002 (pass); **N_eff = 31.11 for Z = 29 (+7.3 %) fails the 5 % check**, pinned in the test. Power-law segments between the same knots, the separately labelled source-side comparison, pass: N_eff = 27.80 (-4.1 %; the authors' Table 10 value is 27.6, -5 %), P_eff = 0.999. The whole 3.31 electron difference lies above 100 eV (+0.87 from 100 eV to 1 keV, +2.07 from 1 to 10 keV, +0.37 above), where the knots are a few per decade across the L and K edges and chords overshoot; it is the representation, not the data. The trapezoid rule gives N_eff = 29.57 (+1.95 %), printed only |also within 5 % | Facts, cited, under operator ruling #34 | 2026-10-07 |
| Optical energy-loss function ELF = Im[-1/ε] of glassy carbon, far infrared to x-ray (100 points, 1.0e-2 eV to 3.0e4 eV) | `validation/data/optical/c_elf_hagemann1975.toml` | Experimental measurement (optical constants n, k), transcription read from an open copy and checked against the scanned tables | As the Al row, with `C/nk/Hagemann.yml` (same commit, fetched 2026-10-08) and the report's **Table 8, "Optical constants of C"** (PDF pp. 58-59 of the same scan, SHA-256 `f2643a91539506dd6794dde3b4810b91c3c7a24a57f98cf8b8b810e8e0931ed9`, seen 2026-10-08). The material is **glassy carbon**: report Section 4, "Glassy carbon (Figs. 27-30, Table 8)", p. 20 (PDF p. 24). Table 8 has 100 rows and the transcription the same 100. **Second read** (`secondread_hagemann1975.json`): n and k read by eye from 200 ppi renders at 17 energies (8, 15, 16, 18, 20, 26, 30 and 40 eV across the valence peak, 160 eV, 290, 300, 310 and 320 eV across the K edge, 500 eV, 1, 5 and 30 keV) all equal the transcription; our ELF agrees with the printed ENERGYLOSS within 0.4 % at all 17. **Joins** (report p. 20; the authors' references in brackets): reflectance [21, 50] by Kramers-Kronig analysis to 0.5 eV, k from reflectance at several angles [51] to 80 eV, transmission [52] to 700 eV ("the absolute values have been reduced by a factor of 0.75 (density correction) to obtain the correct value of 2 effective K-electrons"), Hubbell's compiled cross sections above 700 eV; n then by Kramers-Kronig. **"The values reported here are given on the basis of ρ = 1.5 g cm⁻³"** (p. 20), so the backscatter input `eta_c.toml` sets the target to 1.5 g/cm³ (not the element table's graphite 2.267) to keep the elastic and inelastic paths on one density. The authors also note that carbon optical constants depend on the form (graphite, glassy, evaporated amorphous) and preparation. Converted by `ingest_hagemann.py` as for Al. **Sum rules** (`lindhard/tests/optical_sumrule.rs`, density 1.5 g/cm³, Z/A 0.49954 from the NIST X-ray mass attenuation Table 1, read 2026-10-08): P_eff = 0.991 (linear) and 0.987 (power-law) pass; **N_eff fails in both: 7.01 (+16.9 %, linear) and 6.76 (+12.7 %, power-law)**, against the authors' 6.2 (+3.3 %, Table 10). Integrating ε₂ = 2nk from the printed rows (power-law) against the report's own N-EFF column: 2.384 vs 2.317 at 28 eV, 3.790 vs 3.653 at 100 eV, 6.514 vs 5.963 at 1 keV, 6.765 vs 6.139 at 30 keV; so 0.37 of the 0.63 electron excess lies from the K edge to 1 keV (12 printed knots) and a 3 to 4 % excess is already present below 100 eV, which is not explained here. Both failures are pinned in the test | Facts, cited, under operator ruling #34 | 2026-10-08 |

| Energy-loss function ELF = Im[-1/ε] of Si from REELS, 0.5 to 199 eV (`validation/data/optical/si_elf_yang2019.toml`, 297 knots), **digitized from a figure** | `validation/data/optical/si_elf_yang2019.toml`; extraction `validation/data/digitize/yang2019_si_elf.py`; second read `validation/data/digitize/secondread_si_yang2019.json` | Experimental measurement (REELS at 3, 4 and 5 keV) inverted to a bulk ELF by the authors' model analysis, digitized from the published figure | L. H. Yang, K. Tőkési, J. Tóth, B. Da, H. M. Li and Z. J. Ding, "Optical properties of silicon and germanium determined by high-precision analysis of reflection electron energy loss spectroscopy spectra", Phys. Rev. B 100, 245209 (2019), doi:10.1103/PhysRevB.100.245209, **Fig. 7** (PDF p. 10; red "Present" curve, the ELF averaged over the 3, 4 and 5 keV analyses: linear main panel 0-50 eV, log inset 0-200 eV). **Seen 2026-10-09** in the published version deposited at the Library of the Hungarian Academy of Sciences, <http://real.mtak.hu/105943/1/PhysRevB.100.245209.pdf> (17 pages, 3,694,542 bytes, SHA-256 `99a5f2cc92615f2f483f42e122b810d92c96244b6a3cec6663ddddbe71028648`; OpenAlex `is_oa: true`, bronze). **What the curve is** (paper pp. 2-6, 10-11): REELS spectra measured at 3, 4 and 5 keV were fitted by a reverse Monte Carlo simulation in which a parametrized optical ELF is extended into (q, ω) by the full Penn algorithm (bulk) with the Ritchie-Howie surface term; the ELFs from the three energies are "almost the same in the whole energy loss region" (p. 11) and Fig. 7 is their average. It is not a direct optical measurement and depends on that inversion model; lindhard's inelastic model uses the same full Penn algorithm downstream, which is consistent but not independent. Below about 6 eV the authors note the REELS resolution cannot resolve the optical fine structure (p. 12); their curve rises smoothly from about 1e-4. The paper prints no ELF table (its Tables I-III are sum-rule checks), so the values are **digitized**. **Extraction** (`yang2019_si_elf.py`, needs numpy and Pillow; the figure raster is extracted at run time with `pdfimages -f 10 -l 10`, never committed): axes from the tick marks (main panel 18.0 px/eV and 158.9 px per unit of ELF, fit residuals ≤ 0.017 eV and 0.0013; inset 2.25 px/eV and 71.7 px/decade, residuals ≤ 0.006 eV and 0.002 decade); the red stroke's weighted centre per pixel column. **Joins**: 0.5 to 2.75 eV from the log inset (below 3 eV the main panel's stroke lies on the axis and is clipped by the plot area, reading up to 0.008 high), 3 to 24.75 eV from the linear main panel, 25 to 199 eV from the log inset (199 eV is the last full pixel column; the axis ends at 199.5 eV). Knots every 0.25 eV to 24.75 eV (dense across the 16.7 eV plasmon), every 1 eV from 25 eV, every 0.25 eV across the L₂,₃ edge (96 to 104 eV); each knot interpolated between the two nearest column reads, nothing smoothed or extrapolated. Read: plasmon peak 3.75 at 16.75 eV; pre-edge minimum 2.05e-3 at 96.25 eV; post-edge maximum 4.86e-2 at 108 eV. **Digitizing uncertainty** (half a pixel): about 0.003 in ELF from 3 to 25 eV; about 1.6 % from 25 eV and below 3 eV, more where the curve is steep (below 3 eV and at the edge, where the energy uncertainty is ±0.2 eV). **Second read** (`secondread_si_yang2019.json`): (a) the same figure's two panels, where both show the curve: main/inset ratio median 1.039 over every main-panel column from 20 to 49.5 eV (range 0.988 to 1.166, the largest at the right frame, where the main panel's ELF is about 3 px above the axis; the inset is used there); (b) an independent read of the 5 keV curve of **Fig. 6(b)** (same page, its own log axes, 3.74 px/eV, 107 px/decade) at 32 energies from 3 to 198 eV: 26 within the combined digitizing uncertainty, 6 within twice it. Fig. 6(b) is the 5 keV curve, not the average, so part of the difference is the spread between primary energies: Fig. 6(b)/Fig. 7 is 0.87 to 0.95 from 5 to 10 eV (where the three curves of Fig. 6(b) visibly separate), 0.97 to 1.01 across the plasmon, within 1.3 % from 30 to 198 eV except 1.22 at 102 eV on the edge. Manual check: the knots plotted over the figure lie on the red stroke across the peak, the edge and the low-energy rise. **Sum rules** (`lindhard/tests/optical_sumrule.rs`, NIST Si Z/A 0.49848 and 2.330 g/cm³, read 2026-10-09; linear, power-law and trapezoid rules agree to 0.1 % on these dense knots): **P_eff = 0.9135 against the nonconductor target 1 - 1/n(0)² = 0.9143** (n(0) = 3.4155, the value the authors use, p. 6), passes; the authors' own ps-sum is 1.0011 with the 1/n(0)² term (Table II), i.e. 0.9154 for the integral with their ELF extended above 200 eV. **N_eff = 7.66 of 14 (ratio 0.547) fails and is pinned**: the table stops at 199 eV, so the K shell (about 1.84 keV) and the L-shell tail are absent (N_eff 3.13 to 30 eV, 3.50 to 99 eV). The authors' 14.158 (Table II) includes Henke et al. from 200 eV to 30 keV and atomic scattering factors to 10 MeV (p. 13), which are not committed here. **Consumers**: the TPP 2011 IMFP check (row below) passes for Si at 99.5 eV, 992.3 eV and 9897.1 eV (-1.2 %, +1.8 %, +3.6 %). A transport run that needs losses above 199 eV (keV electrons) gets none from this table | Facts, cited, under operator ruling #34 (as the Hagemann ELFs; Crossref licence: APS publisher copyright, no redistribution licence found). Values read from a figure, as the range and sputter-yield sets were; no figure image is stored. Not SRIM-derived; no other code's output | 2026-10-09 |

| Single-pole Penn (SPA) model: `k_F = (3π/4)^(1/3) ω_p^(2/3)`, the dispersion `ω_q² = ω_p² + (1/3)(k_F q)² + q⁴/4`, the cubic coefficient `(π²/48)^(1/3)`, the Jacobian factor `1 + π q²/(6 k_F)` and the nonrelativistic DIIMFP prefactor `1/(π T')` (Hartree units) | `lindhard/src/electron/inelastic/penn.rs` | Computed from a published formula | The algorithm is D. R. Penn, Phys. Rev. B 35, 482 (1987), doi:10.1103/PhysRevB.35.482, **not seen** (closed access; Unpaywall lists no open copy, checked 2026-10-06). Every equation is taken from the SPA as written by Penn and co-authors in H. Shinotsuka, B. Da, S. Tanuma, H. Yoshikawa, C. J. Powell, D. R. Penn, Surf. Interface Anal. 49, 238 (2017), doi:10.1002/sia.6123, eqs. (2)-(5) and (7)-(13), read 2026-10-06 in the NIST author manuscript, PMC5524379 (retrieved through the NCBI E-utilities). The relativistic factor of its eq. (2) is set to 1 (nonrelativistic kinematics). Checked algebraically, not just transcribed: `(π²/48)^(1/3) = (1/3)(3π/4)^(2/3)` (eq. (11) from eq. (8)) and `π/6` from differentiating eq. (8) (eq. (12)); the tests check that the cubic root reproduces the dispersion (`cubic_root_satisfies_the_dispersion`) and that eq. (13) keeps the optical f-sum at every momentum (`loss_function_keeps_the_f_sum_at_every_momentum`, 2e-3). Lindhard (1954) and Ritchie and Howie, Philos. Mag. 36, 463 (1977), doi:10.1080/14786437708244948, are cited for attribution only and were not read. The change of integration order and the closed-form inner integral used for the IMFP and stopping power are our own algebra, checked against the direct DIIMFP integral (`imfp_and_stopping_are_the_integrals_of_the_diimfp`, 1e-5) | Formula, cited; original paper not seen | 2026-10-06 |

Interface Anal. 49, 238 (2017), doi:10.1002/sia.6123, eqs. (18) and (19), read 2026-10-06 (PMC5524379). `P_eff` is reported without eq. (19)'s `n(0)⁻²` term, which the ELF alone does not determine. | Lindhard dielectric function of a free-electron gas at `T = 0` (RPA): `Re ε = 1 + (k_TF²/q²)[1/2 + (A(z-u)+A(z+u))/(8z)]`, `A(s) = (1-s²) ln|(s+1)/(s-1)|`, `z = q/2k_F`, `u = ω/(q k_F)`, `k_TF² = 4k_F/π`; `Im ε = [(k_F²-a²)₊ - (k_F²-(a+q)²)₊]/q³`, `a = ω/q - q/2`; plasmon weight `π/(∂Re ε/∂ω)` | `lindhard/src/electron/inelastic/lindhard_gas.rs` | Computed from a published formula, **verified by tests rather than by a source table** | J. Lindhard, Mat. Fys. Medd. Dan. Vid. Selsk. 28, no. 8 (1954), **not seen** (no open copy found, checked 2026-10-07). The formula is the standard closed form. It was compared with A. V. Latyshev and A. A. Yushkanov, arXiv:1212.6260v1, section 5 (open preprint, PDF pages 13-16 read 2026-10-07; not Lindhard's paper): the integral `B(k, z)` (p. 14), its closed form `b(k, z)` (pp. 14-15) and eq. (5.5″) (p. 15) with `y -> 0` (collision frequency to zero, which the abstract identifies with Lindhard's formula): `Re ε = 1 - (3 x_p²/4k²) b(k, x)` equals our real part to 1e-10 at six `(k_F, q, ω)` points, and the residues of the p. 14 integral at its two poles (inside `[-1, 1]`) equal our `Im ε` to 1e-15 (the printed closed form `b` gives only the real part). Lindhard's own equation numbers are not known. `Im ε` is derived by us from the Fermi golden rule (an elementary Fermi-sphere area; checked against the static and low-`ω` behaviour). The real part is checked **independently of memory** by tests: a Kramers-Kronig integral over our `Im ε` (`real_part_obeys_kramers_kronig_against_the_imaginary_part`, 2e-5 at five `(q, ω)`), the static limit `1 + k_TF²/q²` and the exact static form, the long-wavelength limit `1 - ω_p²/ω²` and the Bohm-Pines dispersion `ω_pl² = ω_p² + (3/5) k_F² q²`, and the f-sum rule `∫ω Im[-1/ε]dω = (π/2)ω_p²` at seven momenta, plasmon included (1e-6). The relation `ω_p² = 4πn`, `k_F = (3π/4)^(1/3) ω_p^(2/3)` is S2017 below eq. (8) | Formula; original paper not seen | 2026-10-07 |

Checks (unit tests in `mermin.rs`): `γ -> 0` reduces to the Lindhard function to 1e-8 (real and imaginary parts, `γ = 1e-11, 1e-13` and exactly zero); `q -> 0` gives the Drude function `1 - ω_p²/(ω² + iγω)`; `ω -> 0` gives `ε_L(q, 0)`; the f-sum `∫ ω Im[-1/ε_M] dω = (π/2) ω_p²` at three `(γ, q)` pairs to 2e-4 (this property was not found in the sources read; it is a numerical check of the transcription); complex series against the direct form to 1e-9 | Formula, cited via the secondary account; Mermin's paper not opened | 2026-10-07 |
| Mermin-ELF (MELF-GOS) model: `Im[-1/ε(q, ω)] = Σ_j (A_j/(ħω_j)²) Im[-1/ε_M(q, ω; ω_j, γ_j)]`, oscillator parameters fitted to the optical ELF through its `q = 0` Drude-Lorentz form `Σ_j A_j E ħγ_j/([(ħω_j)² - E²]² + [E ħγ_j]²)`; DIIMFP, IMFP and stopping power with the kinematics of the Penn models | `lindhard/src/electron/inelastic/mermin.rs` (`MerminPenn`), `lindhard/src/electron/inelastic/mermin_fit.rs` (`fit_mermin_oscillators`, `MerminFit`) | Computed from a published formula; fit procedure ours | The MELF construction and the fit relation are eqs. (3) and (5) of de Vera et al. (2022) (as in the row above). That paper states that the parameters are fitted to the optical spectrum and that the fit's consistency is checked with the Kramers-Kronig and f-sum rules, but gives **no fitting algorithm**; ours is variable projection (non-negative least squares for the amplitudes, Lawson-Hanson active set with Householder QR, Levenberg-Marquardt on `ln ω_j`, `ln γ_j`), written from the textbook descriptions (Lawson and Hanson 1974; Golub and Pereyra 1973; Levenberg 1944 / Marquardt 1963, **none opened**), so it is not a reproduction of any published fit code. The threshold step `Θ(E - E_th)` of eq. (3) is **not implemented** (a fitted Drude tail extends below any real gap; a follow-up could add it). The default start (tallest local maxima of the ELF, width `E/4`) and the default relative weighting (`ELF_i + 0.01 max ELF`) are our heuristics. The fit reports parameters, residuals, rms, `(π/2) Σ A_j` and `Σ A_j/(ħω_j)²` (closed forms of the Drude sum, see the Drude-Lorentz row) against the data's numerical f-sum and `P_eff` (`SumRuleReport`). No fit to measured data is committed or claimed. **Recorded deviation (#300):** the whole tabulated ELF is fitted, inner-shell edges included, where de Vera et al. (2022) fit Mermin oscillators to the outer-shell ELF only (eqs. (1), (3); inner shells by GOS, eq. (2)); and the default 3-oscillator fit of the committed Cu ELF does not pass the sum-rule consistency check of that paper (section 2.1.1, after eq. (5), "The consistency of the fitting procedure is checked by fulfilling the Kramers-Kronig and f-sum rules", re-opened 2026-10-09 on PMC): fit / table f-sum 0.567, `P_eff` 0.670 against 1.0017, which makes the Cu Mermin IMFP 15 to 41 % longer than TPP 2011 (`docs/validation.md`, "Cu: the Mermin IMFP and the default oscillator fit"; pinned in `tests/mermin_inelastic.rs`, `cu_*`). The default is unchanged (decision #306). **Width floor (ours, #307):** each fitted width `ħγ_j` is bounded below by the local knot spacing of the table at `ħω_j` (the larger of the two gaps next to the nearest tabulated energy), because a narrower Lorentzian can sit between two knots where no residual constrains it; without it the 10- and 16-oscillator fits of the Cu ELF reached 6771 and 16467 times the table's f-sum through a sub-meV oscillator between two knots, which the floor removes (`cu_fits_keep_widths_above_the_knot_spacing`). The floor is enforced as a projection of the fitted `ln ħγ_j` onto the bound, at the start and after every accepted step. Fits in which no width falls below the floor are bitwise unchanged; measured for the default fits of the committed Al, Au, Cu and Si ELFs, Cu with 6 oscillators and the synthetic fixtures. The default fit of the committed C ELF **does** change: its start width 0.05 eV at 0.2 eV is raised to the 0.1 eV knot spacing, and the fit ends at (2.000, 5.020, 28.457) eV, weighted rms 0.29194, within about 1e-7 of the unbounded fit (146 iterations instead of 140; `c_default_fit_starts_on_the_floor_and_leaves_it`). The floor does **not** bound the f-sum at 10 and 16 oscillators: there a very wide oscillator (width 4e5 to 8e5 eV) puts 188 and 106 times the table's f-sum above the table, where the default weighting barely sees it, and both fits end unconverged (#311). Tests: recovery of three known oscillators to 1e-3 relative in `A`, `E`, `γ` from a start perturbed by 10 to 20 % (`recovers_three_known_oscillators_from_a_perturbed_start`); bitwise-identical fits on 5 threads; high-energy stopping power within 3 % of the single pole on the synthetic fixture | Formula, cited; algorithm ours; no data | 2026-10-07 |

Tests: `lindhard/tests/electron_elastic_table.rs`; bench: `lindhard/benches/elastic.rs` | Computed from our own solver and published formulas | Inputs: the radial-Dirac solver (elastic-solver rows above), atom number densities from `Material` (atomic-weight and density rows above), and an atomic potential from a caller-supplied `PotentialSource`. Elements combine by the independent-atom approximation (number-density-weighted sum of atomic DCS), stated in the module docs. The cumulative cross section uses the Legendre identity `(2L+1) P_L = P'_{L+1} - P'_{L-1}`, from DLMF 18.9.7 at `lambda = 1/2` with 18.7.9 and 18.9.19 (<https://dlmf.nist.gov/18.9>, read 2026-10-07), also checked against direct quadrature in a unit test. **Potentials.** `SalvatDhfsTable` gives the Salvat et al. (1987) DHFS potentials via `SalvatDhfs::for_element` (Table I row above, Z = 1..92). `ThomasFermiYukawa` is a stand-in Yukawa potential with the Thomas-Fermi length `0.8853 a0 Z^(-1/3)` (screening-lengths row above), not an atomic DHFS potential; every table built from it says `STAND-IN` in its `model` and `provenance`. The default probability-grid settings (logistic step 0.4, refinement tolerance 5e-4) were chosen by measuring the recovered `sigma_tr1` with the stand-in for Z = 1, 6, 14, 29, 79, 92 (module docs) | Computed | 2026-10-07 |
| Furness-McCarthy local exchange potential `V_ex = (1/2)(E - V_st) - (1/2)[(E - V_st)^2 + 4 pi rho]^(1/2)` (hartree units) | `lindhard/src/electron/elastic/corrections.rs` (`exchange_potential`) | Computed from a published formula | J. B. Furness and I. E. McCarthy, J. Phys. B 6, 2280 (1973), doi:10.1088/0022-3700/6/11/021, **not opened** (IOP subscription; Unpaywall lists only a figshare metadata record without files, checked 2026-10-07), so **its own equation number is not cited**. The formula is taken from F. Salvat, Phys. Rev. A 68, 012708 (2003), doi:10.1103/PhysRevA.68.012708, Eq. (2), read 2026-10-07 in the open copy <https://diposit.ub.edu/bitstreams/c14069fc-1d26-424b-8773-3e19b9ad5c9f/download>, and agrees with A. Jablonski, F. Salvat, C. J. Powell, J. Phys. Chem. Ref. Data 33, 409 (2004), Eq. (15), read in the NIST reprint <https://srd.nist.gov/jpcrdreprint/1.1595653.pdf>. Evaluated in the rationalised form `-2 pi rho/(D + sqrt(D^2 + 4 pi rho))` (our algebra; tested against the printed form). No numbers | Formula, cited (secondary) | 2026-10-07 |
| Correlation-polarization potential: Buckingham tail `-alpha/(2(r^2+d^2)^2)`, `d^4 = alpha Z^(-1/3) b_pol^2/2`, Seltzer `b_pol^2 = (E - 50 eV)/16 eV`, join rule `max(V_co, V_pol)` inside the outer crossing; Perdew-Zunger LDA correlation coefficients 0.0311, 0.0584, 0.00133, 0.0084, `beta_0 = 0.1423`, `beta_1 = 1.0529`, `beta_2 = 0.3334` | `lindhard/src/electron/elastic/corrections.rs` | Computed from a published formula | F. Salvat, Phys. Rev. A 68, 012708 (2003), Eqs. (3)-(7) and (9), pp. 012708-3 and -4, read 2026-10-07 (open copy above). Salvat attributes the correlation potential to J. P. Perdew and A. Zunger, Phys. Rev. B 23, 5048 (1981), **not opened** (publisher bot challenge), and Seltzer's recipe to a private communication. **Sign of Eq. (7a)**: printed with an overall minus that makes the two branches discontinuous at `r_s = 1` (+0.0668 vs -0.0668 hartree); we use the continuous sign, which matches the Perdew-Zunger form `A ln r_s + (B - A/3) + (2/3) C r_s ln r_s + (1/3)(2D - C) r_s`, `A = 0.0311`, `B = -0.048`, `C = 0.002`, `D = -0.0116`, `gamma = -0.1423`, reproduced (citing Perdew & Zunger) at <https://www.dsedu.org/courses/dft/xc>, read 2026-10-07; test `correlation_potential_matches_independent_forms`. The ELSEPA paper (Comput. Phys. Commun. 165, 157 (2005)) was not opened and no ELSEPA code was read. The outer cut of the tail (default 50 bohr) and the WKB start rule are our numerical choices, measured in the tests | Formula, cited | 2026-10-07 |

| Published partial-wave `sigma_el`, `sigma_tr1` for C, Si, Cu, Au at 1 and 10 keV (**not included: gap, placeholder fixture**) | `lindhard/tests/data/elastic_reference.toml` (8 cases, every value absent); test `lindhard/tests/electron_elastic_reference.rs` | Gap: source read, contains no table of these values | Jablonski, Salvat & Powell, J. Phys. Chem. Ref. Data 33, 409 (2004), doi:10.1063/1.1595653. Update 2026-10-07 (#93): the NIST reprint <https://srd.nist.gov/jpcrdreprint/1.1595653.pdf> opens (HTTP 200, 43 pages per `pdfinfo`) and was read in full. It is a method and comparison paper: its only numeric tables are Table 1 (databases), Table 2 (models), Table 3 (transport cross sections of He and Hg at 50 and 100 eV), Table 4 (IMFPs for Au50Cu50 at 200 to 2000 eV) and Table 5 (IMFPs for Cu); the cross sections themselves are shown only in figures, and only for other elements: its elastic cross-section comparisons (DCSs in Figs. 2 to 16 at 100, 500, 1000 and 10000 eV; transport cross sections vs energy in Figs. 23 and 24) use H, Al, Ni, Ag, Au and Cm, Figs. 17 to 22 compare with measurements for the gases He, Ne, Ar, Kr, Xe and Hg, and Fig. 27 is Au at 500 and 1000 eV. C, Si and Cu are in none of the cross-section figures; only Au overlaps with the target list. It tabulates no `sigma_el` or `sigma_tr1` for C, Si, Cu or Au at 1 or 10 keV, so there is nothing to enter; reading values off a figure would not be a cited table value. Earlier access attempt: doi.org and pubs.aip.org answer a bot challenge (403); OpenAlex reports `is_oa: false`, `oa_status: closed`, no repository copy; the NIST SRD 64 site (<https://srdata.nist.gov/srd64/>) is an interactive application with no static tables reachable from this host. No value is entered, from memory or otherwise; the test skips and reports every case, and a skip is not a pass. Gap: a reader with access to a table of these values (SRD 64 output for the four elements, or another published table of partial-wave values with DHFS screening) should enter them with table and page per value, and the reference's stated exchange / polarization settings. Also blocking C, Si and Au: the DHFS coefficients (#130). The tolerances (5 % at 1 keV, 2 % at 10 keV) are pinned in the test. Update 2026-10-08 (#93, second pass): a further search for another accessible published numeric table (OpenAlex full-text-title searches for tabulated elastic / transport cross sections of electrons, Dirac partial-wave or ELSEPA, C / Si / Cu / Au, 1 and 10 keV; <https://srdata.nist.gov/srd64/> again answers HTTP 200 but is interactive only) found no paper that I could open with such a table; candidates returned (ECCPA, Comput. Phys. Commun. 2022; a 2024 Dirac EELS-cross-section paper; a 2019 carbon secondary-electron paper) were not verified to contain these values and none is used. Still 0 of 16 values committed; the harness now rejects malformed fixtures and reports INCOMPLETE coverage | Gap | 2026-10-08 |
| Electron surface-barrier and secondary-electron model formulas and switches: `E_SE = E_F + W - B`, `B = 0` (metal) or `E_g` (insulator, `W > E_g`), the Ivanchenko angle `cos β = sqrt((W + B) / (E - E_F + 2B))`, instantaneous momentum `sqrt(B / ΔE)`, momentum-conserving primary deflection, the loss clamp `W <= E - E_F`; step `ΔU = U' - U`, `T = 4s/(1+s)²`, `s = sqrt(1 + ΔU / E cos²θ)`, refraction; inner potential `E_F + Φ` (metal) or `W_v + E_g + χ` with mid-gap `E_F` (insulator); default switches (quantum transmission, refraction, instantaneous momentum and momentum conservation all on) | `lindhard/src/electron/{boundary,secondary,transport}.rs` | Computed from published formulas; code ported from a permissive source (Tier A) | T. Verduin, *Quantum Noise Effects in e-Beam Lithography and Metrology*, PhD thesis, TU Delft (2017), doi:10.4233/uuid:f214f594-a21f-4318-9f29-9776d60ab06c (open access; read 2026-10-07): Eq. 3.86 p. 78 (secondary energy), pp. 78-79 (band gap as binding energy, sub-gap loss to phonons), Eqs. 3.100 and 3.105-3.111 pp. 82-85 (direction model), Eq. 3.136 p. 99 and Fig. 3.28 p. 98 (inner potential), Eqs. 3.137-3.139 pp. 99-100 (refraction), Eqs. 3.140-3.145 pp. 101-102 (transmission). The model is Kieft and Bosch, J. Phys. D 41, 215310 (2008), doi:10.1088/0022-3727/41/21/215310 (not open access; cited through Verduin). O. Renault et al., e-J. Surf. Sci. Nanotech. 4, 431 (2006), doi:10.1380/ejssnt.2006.431 (open, SHA-256 `d352180a351ec46a0393ac954f4aa40ee904601e19383ed360356202fd9aae50`), gives Cu work functions per grain orientation (4.9, 4.6, 4.5 eV) under the PEEM extraction field, not a polycrystalline value. The Tanuma, Powell and Penn IMFP IX manuscript (cited in the next row) prints a Fermi energy `E_F` per element (Table 1) without saying where it comes from, so it is not adopted as a measured Fermi energy. (3) Not reached: J. Phys. F 3, 1738 (1973), doi:10.1088/0305-4608/3/9/016 (Al single-crystal faces) and Br. J. Appl. Phys. 15, 1964, doi:10.1088/0508-3443/15/8/309 (contact-potential work functions) are listed as open, but iopscience.iop.org answered with a bot-check page; G. Greczynski and L. Hultman, Prog. Mater. Sci. 107, 100591 (2020), doi:10.1016/j.pmatsci.2019.100591 (open, CC BY-NC-ND) answered with a bot-check page at the publisher and its DiVA record was not reached; J. Robertson, Eur. Phys. J. Appl. Phys. 28, 265 (2004), doi:10.1051/epjap:2004206, likewise. (4) Si valence band width: L. Ley, S. Kowalczyk, R. Pollak and D. A. Shirley, report LBL-688 (1972), the preprint of Phys. Rev. Lett. 29, 1088 (1972), doi:10.1103/PhysRevLett.29.1088, <https://escholarship.org/uc/item/91x9h2nq>, SHA-256 `e5a621da943cdb6699c1caf5b98f5638bcc36106427d5cca80fbe0c30f941607`, opened 2026-10-08: Table 1 places the bottom feature `Γ1` of crystalline Si at 14.7 eV below the (gold-referenced) Fermi level of an n-type sample, not relative to the valence band maximum, so no width follows from it without a further assumption. (5) SiO2 electron affinity: Robertson and Wallace (next row), Table 1, print only the conduction band offset of SiO2 on Si (3.2 eV); it equals the affinity difference only with no interface dipole (their Section 4.4, manuscript pp. 13-14), so no affinity is derived. (6) The Nebula paper (van Kessel and Hagen 2020, above) was retrieved this time from the TU Delft repository (<http://resolver.tudelft.nl/uuid:d5740b54-6c37-4598-af70-67268d71696e>, SHA-256 `668bb308063af75e50290168c29af8699e7cb08338fc5ce521ed8121582ec72c`): it prints no band parameters. **2026-10-09 search (#149), appended:** (7) Si valence band width: J. R. Chelikowsky and M. L. Cohen, "Electronic structure of silicon", Phys. Rev. B 10, 5095 (1974), doi:10.1103/PhysRevB.10.5095 (bibliographic data checked against Crossref 2026-10-09), read in its preprint LBL-3127 at <https://escholarship.org/uc/item/4jf90532> (OpenAlex lists it as the green open-access copy), 58 pages, SHA-256 `b5fda2b55b22eab658bc14cf3ac4c8a332aa7c72492cf2e89d68aa793326fb3a`, opened 2026-10-09. Table II (preprint p. 38) gives the computed Γ1 at -12.36 eV (energy-dependent nonlocal pseudopotential; -12.53 eV local) with Γ25', the top of the valence band, at 0.00 eV. Table IV (p. 40) repeats these and lists for Γ1 the measured -12.4 ± 0.6 eV (its ref. 6, W. D. Grobman and D. E. Eastman, Phys. Rev. Lett. 29, 1508 (1972), doi:10.1103/PhysRevLett.29.1508, closed access per OpenAlex, not opened) and -12.5 ± 0.6 eV (its ref. 5, Ley et al. 1972, item (4) above, whose preprint gives Γ1 relative to the Fermi level only; the published letter was not opened). The computed 12.36 eV, for which the paper is the primary source, is used as the Si valence band width of the SE-yield runs only (row "Band inputs of the SE-yield validation runs"); the two measurements are secondary listings and serve as the cross-check (12.36 eV lies inside both stated errors). `BAND_DEFAULTS` was **not** changed: Si's valence band width there is still `None`, and adopting this value as a library default is left to a separate change. | Fröhlich LO-phonon channel formulas: emission and absorption inverse mean free paths `λ±⁻¹ = (n + 1 or n)/a₀ · (1/ε∞ - 1/ε₀) · (ħω/2E) · ln[...]`, Bose occupation `n(T)`, angular distribution `dw/dΩ` and its inverse CDF | `lindhard/src/electron/phonon.rs` (`FrohlichPhonon`, `sample_cos_theta`, `cos_theta_cdf`) | Computed from a published formula | Read in two open-access restatements, which agree: Ding, Li, Da, Liu, "Charging effect induced by electron beam irradiation: a review", Sci. Technol. Adv. Mater. 22, 932 (2021), doi:10.1080/14686996.2021.1976597 (CC BY 4.0; full text read via Europe PMC, PMC8592625), Sec. 2.3, Eqs. (14) and (15) (creation and annihilation rates per unit time, divided here by the speed `√(2E/m)`), the text after Eq. (14) (Bose occupation) and Eq. (16) (angle); and Taioli & Dapor, arXiv:2404.07521v5 (2025), Eqs. (33)-(34) (emission inverse mean free path in the same form). The effective mass is the free-electron mass, as in Taioli & Dapor Eq. (33). The inverse CDF in `cos θ` and the cancellation-free logarithms are our own algebra on those equations, checked by the tests. The primary papers (Fröhlich, Adv. Phys. 3, 325 (1954), doi:10.1080/00018735400101213; Llacer & Garwin, J. Appl. Phys. 40, 2766 and 2776 (1969), doi:10.1063/1.1658076 for part II; Fitting & Friemann, Phys. Status Solidi A 69, 349 (1982)) were **not opened**: on 2026-10-07 (#102) Unpaywall reported no open copy of the first two and the publisher pages answered 403. Note: the issue cites Llacer & Garwin as 40, 2776 (Unpaywall: "II. Transmission Secondary Emission from Alkali Halides"); the reference list of Ding et al. cites part I, 40, 2766. Technol. Adv. Mater. 22, 932 (2021), doi:10.1080/14686996.2021.1976597, Sec. 2.3: `ε₀`, `ε∞` in the text after Eq. (14) (no separate citation given there); the two mode energies in the text before Eq. (14), credited to Kuhr & Fitting, J. Electron Spectrosc. Relat. Phenom. 105, 257 (1999) (**not opened**). Mao, J. Zhu, X. Cheng and Z. Wang, Discover Nano 20, 84 (2025), doi:10.1186/s11671-025-04264-0 (open access, CC BY-NC-ND 4.0; only the formula is used, no text or figure), eq. (1), section "Traditional Gaussian model", read 2026-10-07 in the Europe PMC full text (PMC12089565, `fullTextXML`, SHA-256 of the file read `85b469fb1f3070fb9d8b9a2d13a3ccc76d70c548c49a876323fe9dcff356f352`); it attributes the form to Chang (1975), which was **not seen** (open questions). Triple Gaussian: T. Rosa Figueiro, *Process modeling for proximity effect correction in electron beam lithography*, PhD thesis, Univ. Grenoble Alpes (2015), HAL tel-01206934, eq. (72), p. 111 (PDF page 112), the sum-of-Gaussians PSF `1/(1+Σ η_i) [e^(-x²/α²)/(πα²) + Σ η_i e^(-x²/β_i²)/(πβ_i²)]` with two backscatter terms (its "3 Gaussian PSF model", Fig. 61), read 2026-10-07 at <https://theses.hal.science/tel-01206934/file/ROSA-FIGUEIRO_2015_diffusion.pdf> (SHA-256 `594aa0406a52dbe62b54f9b82a81076c920dad8eca3b781be742b4f216d3fe6a`); our names `β = β_1`, `η = η_1`, `γ = β_2`, `ν = η_2`. The annulus integral `e^(-a²/w²) - e^(-b²/w²)` is our own one-line integration, checked against Simpson quadrature in the unit tests. The fit algorithm is our own (module docs). No numbers from either source enter the tree | Formulas, cited | 2026-10-07 |

| Measured SE yield δ(E) of Al, 12 data sets (`se_al_bongeler1993`, `se_al_bronstein1969`, `se_al_bruining1938`, `se_al_czaja1966`, `se_al_kanaya1972`, `se_al_kanter1961`, `se_al_moncrieff1976`, `se_al_philibert1963`, `se_al_reimer1980`, `se_al_shimizu1974`, `se_al_whetten1962`, `se_al_wittry1966`) | `validation/data/se_yield/se_al_*.json` | Experimental measurement, transcribed from a compilation (#149) | D. C. Joy, *A database on electron-solid interactions*, Revision 01-01 (2001), Section 1(a) 'Secondary Electron Yield Data' (cf. Scanning 17, 270 (1995), doi:10.1002/sca.4950170501; Lin and Joy, Surf. Interface Anal. 37, 895 (2005), doi:10.1002/sia.2107, was not opened: closed access, Wiley 403, OpenAlex reports no open copy). Each file is one original measurement set, cited by the compilation's own reference number and reference-list entry, which each file copies. Transcription of the two-column tables by `validation/data/digitize/joy_se_yield_ingest.py` (no figures digitized; values as printed, keV to eV), read twice with different line-splitting rules (`--check`), then the point counts of every file were compared by eye with the document's tables for Al, Cu, Si and Au | Measured values, committed as cited facts under the operator ruling #34. The compilation (a Word document Joy distributed at web.utk.edu/~srcutk/database.doc, host no longer answering) asks users to acknowledge it with that URL; it is not stored here. Read via the Internet Archive capture of 2010 (`https://web.archive.org/web/2010id_/http://web.utk.edu/~srcutk/database.doc`, 488 960 bytes), 2026-10-07. The compilation does not state the angle of incidence of any set, nor, per set, the energy below which an electron counts as secondary (it says 0 to 50 eV is the usual choice, early work 0 to 70 or 100 eV; its reference 20 is flagged 30 to 100 eV). `incidence_deg` is therefore null with a note in every file. Joy states it applies no quality selection and that discrepancies between sets for one material are large (surface contamination, cutoff choice) | 2026-10-07 |

| Band inputs of the SE-yield validation runs (Al, Cu, Au): work-function ranges Al 4.06 to 4.26 eV, Cu 4.53 to 5.10 eV, Au 5.10 to 5.47 eV; valence electrons per atom Al 3, Cu 1, Au 1 | `validation/experiments/se_yield.py` (`BAND`, `METAL_BAND_PROVENANCE`, echoed in the input of every run and in `se_yield_results.json`); read unchanged by `validation/experiments/backscatter.py` for the fast-secondary sensitivity of η (#148, mid work function; echoed in `backscatter_results.json`) | Caller-supplied inputs to the validation runs; no library default (#115 found none that could be cited) | Work function: the range printed for each element in the table 'Work function of elements' of the English Wikipedia article 'Work function', revision 1368612509 (2026-08-10), which cites CRC Handbook of Chemistry and Physics (2008), p. 12-124; the Handbook was not opened, so this is a secondary table. The low and high ends are the table's two numbers and the 'mid' value is their mean (a choice made here, not a measurement). Valence electrons: ground-state configurations in the Wikipedia article 'Electron configuration' (Al [Ne]3s²3p¹, Cu [Ar]3d¹⁰4s¹; Au [Xe]4f¹⁴5d¹⁰6s¹ from that article's table of anomalous configurations, revision 1378968605 of 2026-10-07; Au added 2026-10-08), counted as the conduction electrons of a free-electron metal. The Fermi energy is computed by the library's formula (row above) from these and the element-table density | Facts, cited; not entered from memory. #115 (2026-10-08) found no openable source for these work functions or Fermi energies (row "Per-material barrier parameters"), so they stay | 2026-10-07 |
| Band inputs of the SE-yield validation runs (Si, an insulator band): valence band width 12.36 eV, band gap 1.1 eV, electron affinity 4.05 eV | `validation/experiments/se_yield.py` (`BAND["Si"]`, `SI_BAND_PROVENANCE`, echoed in the input of every Si run and in `se_yield_results.json`) | Band gap and affinity: the cited library defaults (`BAND_DEFAULTS`, #115, row "Cited band defaults"). Valence band width: a published computed value (operator ruling #34), caller-supplied to these runs only | Band gap and affinity: J. Robertson and R. M. Wallace, Mater. Sci. Eng. R 88, 1-41 (2015), doi:10.1016/j.mser.2014.11.001, authors' manuscript hdl:1810/246441, Table 1, p. 43 (gap 1.1 eV) and Fig. 42, p. 63 (affinity 4.05 eV), as `BAND_DEFAULTS` cites them. Valence band width: the separation of Γ1 (-12.36 eV) below Γ25' (0.00 eV, the valence band top) in the energy-dependent nonlocal pseudopotential calculation of J. R. Chelikowsky and M. L. Cohen, Phys. Rev. B 10, 5095 (1974), doi:10.1103/PhysRevB.10.5095, Table II, read in the preprint LBL-3127 (escholarship.org/uc/item/4jf90532, SHA-256 `b5fda2b55b22eab658bc14cf3ac4c8a332aa7c72492cf2e89d68aa793326fb3a`, preprint p. 38), opened 2026-10-09. Cross-check in the same paper, Table IV, p. 40: photoemission gives Γ1 at -12.4 ± 0.6 eV (Grobman and Eastman 1972, UPS) and -12.5 ± 0.6 eV (Ley et al. 1972, XPS); those letters were not opened as published (row "Per-material barrier parameters", items (4) and (7)). The Fermi energy the library derives from these (W_v + E_g/2 = 12.91 eV) only sets the `cutoff-band-bottom` threshold, 13.91 eV | Facts, cited; not entered from memory. The computed value was preferred over the two measured ones because only for it is the opened paper the primary source | 2026-10-09 |
